Aug 26, 2026, 10-Q 224“discouraged” kept; sales under the American licences for the H20 and the H200 “were restricted by the PRC government”both kept; the second now reads “for wide distribution in China's data center market with the approval from both the USG and the Chinese government”

Of the latest annual reports of fifteen US-listed Chinese companies read for this note, only Alibaba's says a Chinese authority asked Chinese technology companies to stop buying certain chips: “In September 2025, the Cyberspace Administration of China requested Chinese technology companies to halt purchases of certain chips.” 245 It does not name Nvidia. It follows the words “On the other hand” and a sentence in which the regulator, in May 2023, “banned the use of its products from key infrastructure projects in China”, the products of an American memory-chip maker 245; the published review conclusion, on the regulator's site, told operators of critical information infrastructure to stop buying them, “应停止采购美光公司产品” (shall stop buying Micron's products) 246 (point 18). Both sentences stand in a passage on American export controls, and the next sentence says such restrictions “have affected and may have further material adverse effects” on Alibaba's access to technology and computing 245. Alibaba's Chinese annual report for the same year, dated May 20, 2026, gives its risk factors only in summary, “風險因素摘要載列如下” (a summary of the risk factors is set out below), and carries neither sentence; its note on export controls gives that passage's general sentences without its examples 247. A search of the other fourteen found no such sentence (the same search finds Alibaba's); where they speak of chip supply they name export controls, as Baidu's does: “如果我們因出口管制而無法採購足夠數量的先進半導體芯片” (if export controls leave us unable to buy enough advanced chips) 248. Two Chinese makers of AI chips, answering the Shanghai exchange's listing inquiries in August and September 2025, explained the H20's prospects by the cyberspace regulator's summons and, Moore Threads, by “美国出口管制政策的不停变化、市场对于其芯片是否存在安全问题的担忧” (the constant changes in US export-control policy, and the market's worry whether its chips are safe) 249, or, MetaX, by “我国对高端算力芯片安全可控的战略方针” (the country's strategic line that high-end computing chips be secure and controllable), adding that national public AI computing platforms have explicit requirements to deploy home-made computing 250; neither says that an authority told buyers to stop or to justify their orders, and both say their own chips can take the H20's place. Qualcomm says some of its customers in China, makers of phones and other devices, have developed their own chips or use rivals' in place of its own, and that others may, for reasons among which it lists “pressure, encouragement or incentives from, or policies of, the Chinese government” and concerns over losing access “as a result of actual, threatened or potential U.S. or Chinese government actions” (annual report, Nov 5, 2025) 251. AMD, May 6, 2026, of the chips Washington licensed, which its filing calls MI325 products: “We do not yet know whether any imports of MI325 products will be allowed into China” 252; the sentence is gone from its report of Nov 4, 2026, with no reason given 253. On results calls the Chinese companies said less: in the 76 results-call transcripts of eight Chinese companies, November 2023 to November 2026, and 42 records of four smaller model makers read for this note, no speaker and no record names a Chinese authority as restricting chip purchases, though 27 of the transcripts leave out passages spoken in another language (an earlier count of 25 read the pages' plain text, which drops a paragraph that holds only the marker); in Chinese records of 25 of those 27 calls (14 of them call transcripts that keep the Chinese passages; none was found for Kingsoft Cloud's calls of May 28 and Aug 20, 2025) no speaker names a Chinese authority beside a chip word either. In the transcript of the stock-data site Stock Analysis, the speaker it labels as Tencent's chief strategy officer, May 13, 2026, put a shortage of chips down to “a combination of policy restrictions on certain, you know, foreign design GPUs being brought into China” and to limited factory capacity for Chinese designs 254. The answer does not say whose policy. Yicai's Chinese report of the same answer names no speaker and carries only the part about China's own chips, “国内的GPU和ASIC算力一直很紧张，但目前这个问题正得到逐步解决，国内的晶圆厂正在对产能进行补充。” (GPU and ASIC compute in China has been tight, but the problem is now gradually being solved, and China's fabs are adding capacity), and that the company had met no artificial additional limits on CPU and network chips 255. Sina Tech's Chinese record of the call's questions and answers carries the import half as well, under the same speaker name: “一方面是对部分海外生产、设计的GPU有政策性限制，这部分GPU无法进入中国市场” (on the one hand, policy restrictions on some GPUs produced and designed abroad, which cannot enter the Chinese market) 256. Another Chinese transcript, on Xueqiu, puts the same answer under the chief financial officer, “发言人：罗硕瀚” 257; Tencent's own replay was not reachable, so who spoke is not settled.

12American officials, on the record. The Commerce Secretary, Howard Lutnick, to a Senate subcommittee, Apr 22, 2026 258: “my understanding is their cloud companies want to buy these chips”, and the “Chinese central government has not let them as of yet buy the chips because they're trying to keep their investment focused on their own domestic industry” 259. The US Trade Representative, Jamieson Greer, May 26, 2026: “my own sense is that what passes for the private sector in China is interested in the highest technology chips they can get. And the government in China and the party are interested in promoting their domestic production of chips and indigenous innovation.”; “And you can guess who wins in that situation.” 260 On Nov 20, 2026, in a Bloomberg interview (machine captions, not checked by ear; 1:06 to 1:35), he said the Chinese side is “not as monolithic as people would have you believe”: the private sector is “quite eager to get the most advanced chips they can”; by his “own analysis” some “think, well, we'd like to have access”, others “we don't want American chips anyway” and “We want to see the ascend chips expand their market” 261. A second caption track differs on one word and on a sentence break, so whether he puts the split inside the government needs a listen 262. The President, Donald Trump, May 15, 2026, asked whether advanced chips such as the H200 had come up with Xi, began “It didn't come up, but, as you know, Jensen was there.” and went on, of the H200, “China needs it. And so, yes, it came up.”; asked “But they haven't bought any so far?”, he said: “No, because they chose not to. They want to try and develop their own. But it did come up, and I think something could happen on that.” 263 On Oct 29, 2025, when, after a question about exports of “Nvidia Blackwells or downgraded chips”, a reporter put it to him that China had discouraged purchases of those chips, he said: “Well, China actually terminated the relationship with the Nvidia, yes.” 264 The next day he said “We did discuss chips”, and “they're going to be talking to Nvidia and others about taking chips”, and that he had said it was “really between you and Nvidia”, with Washington, in his words, “sort of the arbitrator”; asked whether that meant downgraded Blackwell chips, he said “No, no, not the Blackwell.”, and the Trade Representative, speaking beside him, added: “The Blackwell chips, of course, weren't discussed.” 265 The Treasury Secretary, Scott Bessent, Nov 20, 2026: “I think what we have seen is a paucity of orders from the Chinese on the H200s as they try to build out their own chip stack” 266. The House Select Committee on China has said both things, each time citing a news report. In a report it released on Apr 16, 2026: “Beijing subsequently approved H200 purchases by Alibaba, Tencent, and ByteDance”, citing Reuters 267268. On Nov 13, 2026: “the PRC government refused the H200 chips, instead pushing its own homegrown industry”, citing an article on the President's words of May 15 269270. The Commerce Secretary, the Treasury Secretary and the Trade Representative each give theirs as their own understanding or view. The Commerce Secretary and the Trade Representative set the companies apart from the government; the President and the Treasury Secretary do not. And Senator Chris Coons challenged the Commerce Secretary's account. His letter of May 5, 2026 says Nvidia's chief executive, Jensen Huang, had told reporters that March that Nvidia “had received necessary approvals from both the United States and PRC governments”, and: “Your statements before the committee appear to contradict Huang's comments.” 271

Reported, from unnamed people

13Guidance from 2024. The Information reported on May 13, 2024, by two people who work with the companies, that officials had told ByteDance, Tencent, Alibaba and Baidu “to cut back on their purchase of foreign-made artificial intelligence chips in favor of buying more domestically made chips”, and that “the Chinese government now wants tech companies to buy equal numbers of domestic and foreign-made AI chips for new internet data centers”; by three people in the talks, firms ordering more foreign chips “will have to detail in writing how many U.S. chips they have ordered for deployment and justify the decision”; the article adds, in its own words, “The new guidance hasn’t yet been strictly enforced” 272 (the article as a publisher's awards file reproduces it; the paper's own page is behind its wall). Reuters, May 24, 2024, in a report that cites three unnamed people in the supply chain, says in its own words: “Another major stumbling block to the success of Nvidia's H20 chip in China has been a directive by Beijing for companies to buy Chinese chips, although two of the three sources said those orders had eased in recent months.” By the same report's three sources, the H20 was in “abundant supply”, and by two of them it sold “in some cases at an over 10% discount to Huawei's Ascend 910B”; in government procurement data, which it calls “not exhaustive”, “just five state or state-affiliated buyers” had shown interest in buying the H20 over six months, “compared with over a dozen for Huawei's 910B”; and, by two of the sources, Alibaba had ordered “over 30,000 H20 chips” 273. Bloomberg, Sept 27, 2024 (carried by The Business Times on Sept 28, Singapore time), by people it did not name: “Chinese regulators have been discouraging companies from purchasing Nvidia's H20 chips” 274. The same report gives, by people familiar with China's AI policy, a limit to that guidance: officials also wanted companies to “build the best AI systems possible”, and “If that means they need to buy some foreign semiconductors over domestic alternatives, Beijing will still tolerate that”; and, by one of its people, some companies were “turning a blind eye to the Chinese decree to avoid H20 chips and rushing to buy more of them” ahead of an expected American sanction, while also “buying homemade Huawei chips to please Beijing” 274.

14The summer of 2025. The earliest press reports of the summer's instructions among those saved for this project came on Aug 12, 2025. Bloomberg, by people it did not name: “Over the past few weeks, Chinese authorities have sent notices to a range of firms discouraging use of the less-advanced semiconductors”; the guidance “was particularly strong against the use of H20s for any government or national security-related work”; and, in Bloomberg's own words, “Beijing has stopped short of outright banning the hardware” 275. The Financial Times, the same day, by three people: regulators such as the industry ministry had asked companies “to explain why they need to order Nvidia's H20 chips instead of using domestic alternatives”, and, by two of the three, some of the main buyers “were planning to downsize their orders”; a data-centre operator said: “It's not banned but has kind of become a politically incorrect thing to do” 276. That article is held only as a third-party mirror of the copy Ars Technica carried, not from the paper's own site. Reuters, in a copy carried the next day, by three people: the companies “have not been ordered to stop buying H20 chips” 277. The same copy relays a report by The Information, which Reuters said it “could not immediately confirm”, that ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent “had been ordered by the CAC in the past two weeks to suspend Nvidia chip purchases altogether” 277. Two reports give the stop as lasting until a security check: The Epoch Times (Hong Kong), Aug 13, 2025, in a compiled Chinese report that gives The Information's report first, says that, by two people, the regulators had said purchases of new Nvidia chips must be suspended until their security risks were investigated, “在調查輝達向中國市場銷售的晶片的潛在安全風險之前，必須暫停採購輝達新的晶片” (until the potential security risks of the chips Nvidia sells to the Chinese market are investigated, purchases of Nvidia's new chips must be suspended) 278; and The Wall Street Journal, Aug 23, 2025, as Mint carries it, says in its own words of the H20: “The government instructed Chinese companies not to buy them until the all-clear was given.” 279 The Financial Times named three bodies on Aug 20, 2025, citing people “with knowledge of the regulatory action”: “the Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC), the National Development and Reform Commission (NDRC) and the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology (MIIT) moved in response to comments made by Lutnick last month” 172. Howard Lutnick is the American Commerce Secretary; he had said on television that China was not sold America's “best stuff”, and by the paper's sources some Chinese leaders found that “insulting” 172. About a week after those remarks of Jul 15, by the paper's account, the cyberspace regulator issued what the paper calls “window guidance”, “an informal notice”, “instructing them to halt new orders for Nvidia's H20 chips”; the industry ministry “also spoke with Chinese tech executives informally to reinforce CAC's stance”; and the state planner asked them to refrain from “purchasing all Nvidia chips, including the H20” 172. “As a result, Chinese tech groups held off or significantly downsized their H20 orders”, the paper wrote, “according to those with knowledge of their plans” 172. The same article gives a second reason: “Some Chinese tech companies have held off H20 orders because they want to see if the China-specific Blackwell chip, which potentially has better performance, would become available” 172. The Wall Street Journal, Jul 23, 2026, looking back: “Authorities privately told companies to halt their Nvidia purchases, while officials publicly raised cybersecurity concerns about the U.S. company's products.” By people familiar with the message, Vice Premier Ding Xuexiang warned China's largest AI users, in the paper's words: “Anyone who resisted employing domestic chips was a traitor.” And of January 2026: “Beijing told some companies they could buy the H200s only when necessary, while demanding a commitment to use more domestic chips.” 280 In public, Ding paired self-reliance with openness: at the Digital China Summit on Apr 29, 2026, by Xinhua's report, he said “我國人工智能隻有走自主創新發展之路，才有應對任何遏制打壓的底氣” (only by taking the road of development through independent innovation will China's AI have the confidence to meet any containment or suppression) and that its development “不能筑起封閉的圍牆，而應搭建互利合作的橋梁” (must not build closed walls, but should build bridges of mutually beneficial cooperation) 281.

15Orders, customs and data centres. Reuters, Sept 16, 2025: “Beijing has become increasingly keen for Chinese firms to switch to domestic chips even if Nvidia's products are preferred by many companies” 282. By three people the Financial Times did not name, the cyberspace regulator “told companies, including ByteDance and Alibaba, this week to end their testing and orders of the RTX Pro 6000D”, Nvidia's other chip made for China (Financial Times, Sept 17, 2025) 283; Bloomberg reported the instruction the same day from people of its own 284. An executive at one of the companies told the paper: “The message is now loud and clear”; “Now it's all hands on deck to build the domestic system.” 283 “Teams of customs officers have been mobilised at major ports across the country in the past few weeks to carry out stringent checks on semiconductor shipments” (Financial Times, Oct 9, 2025, by three people it did not name) 285. By two sources Reuters did not name, new data-centre projects that took state money were told “to only use domestically-made artificial intelligence chips” (Reuters, Nov 5, 2025), which added: “It is unclear whether the guidance applies nationwide or only to certain provinces, sources said” 286. Reuters reported on Jan 8, 2026, as The Business Times carries it, by one person it did not name, that “Beijing in recent days asked some Chinese tech companies to temporarily pause their H200 chip orders as regulators are still deciding how many domestically produced chips each customer will need to buy alongside each H200 order” 287. Later in January 2026, by people Reuters did not name, customs agents were told that the H200 chips “are not permitted to enter China”, and companies were “explicitly instructed not to purchase the chips unless necessary”; Reuters wrote that it was unclear whether Beijing wanted to ban the chip outright, “is still chewing over restrictions, or whether these measures could be used as a bargaining tactic in talks with Washington” 288. Leiphone, Oct 31, 2025, described a newly circulating document on window guidance (窗口指导, the same term as the Financial Times's window guidance in point 14): “有最新流传的‘窗口指导’相关文件写道”. By its account, projects “有补贴等地方力量参与” (in which local forces such as subsidies take part) “应全部使用国产芯片” (are to use home-made chips only); projects not far along “可要求对国外芯片进行拆除” (may be required to remove foreign chips); “新建的市场化项目，则被明确禁止使用H20等国外减配版GPU” (new market-based projects are expressly barred from the H20 and other cut-down foreign GPUs); and local projects outside the national hub clusters get no subsidies 289. No copy of the document was found: the article gives no issuer, number or date, and neither web searches for its words, such as “应全部使用国产芯片”, nor the titles on the state planner's and the finance ministry's lists of documents turned one up. The state planner's Li Chao, of its policy research office, did speak in public the same day, using the same term for the hub clusters: new large and very large computing facilities are to be sited first within the “国家枢纽算力设施集群” (national hub computing clusters); that answer has no rule on chips 290. The official texts found that describe the window guidance of 2025 give it as a rule of the state planner, with the industry ministry and the data administration, on approving, siting and funding computing centres, with no rule on chips: a city's reply of June 2025, “2025年4月，国家、省相继下发新政策，要求对算力中心建设实施‘窗口指导’” (in April 2025 the state and the province, one after the other, issued new policies requiring window guidance for building computing centres) 291, and, by the same reply, “算力设施建设由其他社会资金出资建设的不纳入‘窗口指导’范围，采用项目备案制度” (computing facilities built with other social funds are not within the scope of the window guidance and come under project filing) 291; a city data bureau's Party group names the three bodies, “根据国家发展改革委、数据局、工信部联合对算力中心实施‘窗口指导’的要求” (by the requirement of the National Development and Reform Commission, the data administration and the industry ministry, which jointly apply window guidance to computing centres) 292; a province's data bureau, Liaoning's, in a reply of Jun 24, 2026, says “当前算力中心已被列为‘两高’项目并受国家发改委‘窗口指导’政策严格约束，明确规定地方政府不得对算力建设环节进行任何财政补贴政策” (computing centres are now listed as “two highs” projects, high in energy use and emissions, and are strictly bound by the state planner's window-guidance policy, which expressly bars local governments from any fiscal subsidy for building computing) 141; and a city plan names the notice, “《关于对算力基础设施实施‘窗口指导’的通知》（发改高技〔2025〕320号）” 293. An industry body gives the guidance a point on chips: the data-centre committee of the China Communications Industry Association, in a report of 2025 it compiled with companies and others, lists among China's important computing policies the window guidance for computing infrastructure projects, of March 2025, with the state planner, the industry ministry and the data administration as its issuers, as covering “已建成、在建、拟建算力中心” (computing centres built, being built and planned) and as asking that “算力中心需明确采用芯片的类型、数量、国产化率等指标” (a computing centre must state such indicators as the type, number and home-made rate of the chips it uses) 294; that is a summary, not the notice's text, and as summarised it sets no share and bars no chip.

16The result. Reuters, May 14, 2026, by three people it did not name: Washington had cleared about ten Chinese firms to buy the H200, but “not a single delivery has been made so far”, and, by one person, “Chinese firms pulled back after guidance from Beijing”; the same person said the shift “was partly triggered by changes on the U.S. side, though exactly what changed remains unclear”, and Reuters wrote that the “path to a completed sale has been obstructed by a tangle of requirements on both sides” 295. Reuters, Nov 5, 2026, by two people it did not name: “About 600,000 of Nvidia's advanced artificial intelligence chips are stored in Hong Kong awaiting shipment to China”, “as Beijing withholds approval for large-scale imports despite demand from technology firms” 296. Luckey's 600,000 matches this report. What Washington did with the licence applications is on the record, not reported. The table of licence applications for the H200 and AMD's comparable MI325X, with their status on May 11, 2026, from the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS), was made public on Nov 19, 2026 by the House Foreign Affairs Committee, under its chairman's powers and by the committee's “unanimous consent” 297298. The table marks every approved application “Approved, not shipped”, and 1,888,950 of the 3,444,160 chips in it, 55%, are in applications returned without action 5 (the table by status is in section 4).

The returned applications include every one for more than 75,000 chips and all 1,118,500 chips meant for places in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand; no approved application is for more than 75,000. The committee adds that some applications are “not fully reflected in the May 11, 2026, summary document”: “For example, NVIDIA applied on December 19, 2025, to export 445,000 H200s to Tencent” 6; the table shows an Nvidia application of that date for Tencent at 75,000 chips, approved, its only Tencent row of that date; neither document says whether that row is the application of 445,000. So the 55% is a share of the table, not of everything applied for: it would be 49.5% if that row is the application, counted at 445,000, and 48.6% if the 445,000 is a further application and was not returned without action (my arithmetic). The table gives no reasons; RWA is the bureau's own short form: “If your original application was returned without action (RWA)” 185. Each row names a data-centre operator or ultimate consignee: an application is not an order, and an approval here is Washington's licence, not Beijing's. Below its rows the table adds that BIS “has granted two licenses which authorize various chips, including the H200, to go to Lenovo, but which do not authorize the chips to go to China locations” 5; they are not among the 54 rows.

4. The numbers

The rows count different things and are not one series. Four are on the record: the United States government's count (two rows) and Nvidia's own account (two). The rest come from press reports citing unnamed people, and two later accounts say otherwise of the first: Nvidia's chief executive, the day after the report of the approvals in the fourth row, said “我认为中国政府仍在决策中，我们尚未收到采购订单” (I think the Chinese government is still deciding; we have not yet received purchase orders), as Voice of America's Chinese service renders him 299, and TechWeb, as Sina carries it, reported on Jul 17, 2026, from industry insiders it did not name, that “国内AI厂商几乎未对英伟达H200芯片下达批量采购订单” (China's AI firms had placed almost no bulk purchase orders for Nvidia's H200) 300. An application in Washington's table names a data-centre operator or ultimate consignee; it is not an order, and approved there means an American licence, not a Chinese one.
