The sums are this note's, of the table's 54 rows read from its page images; they match the summary's ten figures 56. The table marks returns RWA, “returned without action (RWA)” on the export bureau's pages 185, and gives no reason; one possible reason is that “The applicant has requested the application be returned” 306. The committee says some applications are “not fully reflected” in BIS's summary of May 11, 2026, such as Nvidia's of Dec 19, 2025 “to export 445,000 H200s to Tencent” 6; the table's only Tencent row of that date is for 75,000, approved, and the documents do not say whether it is that application (point 16).

Nvidia's share in China. Reuters, in its own words: “Before U.S. export curbs tightened, Nvidia commanded about 95% of China's advanced chip market.” 295 By the research firm IDC's data and its own sales, as the chip maker Enflame combines them in its prospectus of September 2026, about 4 million AI accelerator cards were shipped in China in 2025, Nvidia's 2.2 million of them, about 55%: “英伟达以约220万张出货量占据约55%的市场份额” 159. By Reuters's account of IDC's data for 2025, which has the same total and the same figures for Nvidia, Chinese makers were “accounting for 41% of the total market” 307308. For January to June 2026 IDC gives Nvidia “its 34% market share” and Huawei “a 28% share of the overall market” 309. TrendForce, as reported in June 2026, counts differently: the foreign suppliers' combined share “is expected to fall to 21 percent in China this year, from 34 percent last year” 310. Bernstein, by the Associated Press's report of Jun 29, 2026, “estimated that Nvidia had about a 40% market share in China's AI chips market in 2025, roughly matched by Huawei” 311, where IDC's data give 55%. These are different measures, not one series. Huawei's rotating chairman, Eric Xu, gave a reading with no figure at a media roundtable on Sept 17, 2026, in Huawei's own English summary: “Right now, it's pretty hard to collect data about the market share of NVIDIA in China. But based on the data we have collected, Ascend has surpassed NVIDIA.” 312 The Chinese summary is more guarded, “昇腾应该已经超过了英伟达” (Ascend should already have passed Nvidia) 313, and Reuters, in its report as The Edge Malaysia carries it, quoting him as saying “I think Ascend market share should be bigger than Nvidia's”, wrote: “Huawei did not provide data to support the estimate.” 314 In the same session he said Huawei did not have “enough capacity to even satisfy the demand in China”, and of its supply, already “supply at scale” by Huawei's past, set against the demand for AI infrastructure: “I think it is very little” 312.

A Chinese chip maker's revenue. Cambricon's filings show the other side of the same years: its revenue was 25.7 million yuan in the first quarter of 2024 and 3,530.8 million yuan in the third quarter of 2026 315316. Its five largest customers, whom it does not name, took 88.66% of its sales in 2025 317.

Nvidia's own revenue from customers based in China. This is from Nvidia's reports to the Securities and Exchange Commission, not from the press. Nvidia counts revenue by where its direct customer has its headquarters. For China, Hong Kong included, the line was $9,659 million in the quarter ended Apr 27, 2025 244, $2,973 million in the quarter ended Oct 26, 2025 4, $7,880 million in the quarter ended Jul 26, 2026 224 and $11,050 million in the quarter ended Oct 25, 2026 318. So it fell by about two thirds during 2025 and then rose: the latest quarter is the highest of the nine reported on this basis, and 9.5% of Nvidia's revenue of $115,844 million (the division is mine). The two fourth quarters in that run of nine are my subtraction of nine-month figures from annual ones 2434. The line is not a count of chips entering China. Nvidia's note on the table: “The end customer and shipping location may be different from our direct customers' headquarters location.” 318 It covers every product; the then interim chief executive on CNBC, Nov 17, 2026 (Nvidia's Board reinstated the chief executive on Nov 22, 2026 319): “Our shipments to China are approximately about 10 percent. But that is our gaming business. That is our automotive business. That is our workstation business that is going there.” 320 For the next quarter the filing says: “We are not assuming any Data Center compute revenue from China in our outlook.” 318 Point 19 gives Nvidia's words on its call.

Customs figures. China's imports under the customs line for parts and accessories of computers were US$82.2 billion in January to September 2026, against US$18.9 billion a year earlier 321322. The line is a total for a kind of goods and names no maker, so it cannot count Nvidia chips. Nvidia's own list puts its H200 NVL and H20 NVL16 parts in subheading 8473.30, parts and accessories of computers, and its HGX H200 and HGX H20 parts in 8471.80, which this table does not show on a line of its own 323.

5. Five limits on the claim

17Beijing was not one actor. Vice Premier He Lifeng told Nvidia's chief executive in April 2025 that more American firms, Nvidia among them, were welcome to put down deeper roots in China: “欢迎包括英伟达公司在内的更多美资企业深耕中国市场” 324. The commerce minister told him in July 2025 that he hoped multinationals, Nvidia among them, would give Chinese customers high-quality, reliable products and services: “希望包括英伟达在内的跨国公司为中国客户提供优质可靠的产品和服务” 325. Nvidia's chief executive, asked in August 2025 about China's scrutiny of the H20, said, by the Wall Street Journal's report as Mint carries it: “They requested and urged us to secure licenses for the H20s for some time.” 279 That is his account, and he does not say who asked. The Financial Times, Nov 13, 2026, describes the split: the commerce ministry “favoured imports”, while security officials in the cyberspace regulator and the security ministry “viewed dependence on Nvidia as a strategic vulnerability”; “Over the past year, the latter position has gained ground.” 326 No other outlet was found reporting this split from sources of its own; the paper had described the like on Aug 20, 2025 172. The US Trade Representative said in November 2026 that the Chinese side is not monolithic (point 12). In the same days as the first of the summer's instructions (point 14), People's Daily Overseas Edition (Jul 22, 2025) printed an interview in which a university scholar, Zhang Xin, advised that “国内企业的最优策略是‘双线并行’” (the best strategy for Chinese firms is two lines at once): “一边积极申请、储备进口高端芯片” (on one side, actively apply for and stock imported high-end chips), on the other fill the computing gap with innovation in algorithms and the home-made ecosystem 327. That is a scholar's printed advice, not an act of the state. On May 14, 2026, meeting the American executives who came with the President, Xi said: “中方欢迎美国对华加强互利合作，相信美国企业在华将拥有更广阔前景” (China welcomes more mutually beneficial American cooperation and believes American firms will have broader prospects in China) 18. Premier Li Qiang told them, Nvidia's among them, that a stable and open policy environment is the Chinese government's long-term commitment that will not change: “一个稳定开放的政策环境是中国政府长期不变的承诺” 328. Reuters, Jan 30, 2026, reporting that DeepSeek too had been approved: “China's industry and commerce ministries have granted approvals for all four companies” (DeepSeek, ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent), with the conditions left to the state planner 329. By The Information's report of Sept 27, 2026, which Reuters relayed and could not verify, it was the industry ministry that told some companies the government intends to approve their purchases of the RTX PRO 5500 (point 19) 330. The commerce ministry also proposed to ease Chinese firms' computing abroad, inside a new control: presenting on Oct 16, 2026 a draft that adds 23 AI technologies, computing clusters among them, to the catalogue of technologies whose export is prohibited or restricted, it said exports of technology for “中国企业在境外自建自营算力中心” (computing centres that Chinese firms build and run themselves abroad) would get “适用自动许可等便利措施” (automatic licences and other facilitation) 331, while the announcement of the commerce and science ministries that carries the draft at the same time put the technologies it lists under a temporary registration before export, “并同步对相关技术实施出口前登记管理” (and at the same time applies registration before export to the technologies concerned) 332, and the draft's items on computing clusters are for clusters with “人工智能加速卡总数超过10,000张” (more than 10,000 AI accelerator cards in all) 333; and on Nov 17, 2026 it called the new American curbs on remote access interference in the “正常贸易合作” (normal trade cooperation) of Chinese firms with firms of other countries 334. Local voucher rules paid for computing that is not home-made too: of the 91 rules found by searches that went province by province and city by city, saved and read and counted (not a census of every local government), 80 state no higher rate for home-made computing and 7 pay a lower rate than for home-made; 4, Sichuan's, Ningbo's, Huangyan district's and the Changsha zone's, pay only for home-made (point 3). Nine of those 80, though, also give home-made computing money to build, buy or run its facilities, an award for adapting to it, or a condition on suppliers, by another provision of the same rule: a premium beside a payment that stays open to other computing (point 3). Some state-linked bodies named Nvidia's chips for computing projects in their own words: Sinomach, which Reuters calls a state-owned engineering group 335, carried on its site on Feb 11, 2025 its design institute's word that the institute had won a 3-billion-yuan computing-centre project in Xi'an that is to use “英伟达H100、H200算力芯片” 336, and an undated listing of a county's investment service centre on the Fujian commerce department's investment site says the computing centre that is the first of two phases of a project seeking investors will have its computing servers “全部采用H100的服务器” 337; neither page says how the chips would be obtained. Local governments' own pages, too, name Nvidia's chips for computing centres and platforms, mostly beside home-made ones: Haidong's city government office, in Qinghai, in the plan for a zero-carbon computing park it issued on Apr 22, 2025, lists a centre of “128 台 H800GPU 服务器” (128 H800 GPU servers) 338; a project that the Jilin commerce department's pages list for investors on Apr 27, 2026 plans “3600P 英伟达 H100 稀疏算力与 2400P 国产算力” (3,600P of Nvidia H100 sparse computing and 2,400P of home-made computing) 339; a news item of Mar 20, 2026 credited to the government of Huangnan prefecture, in Qinghai, has a centre begun on a “‘国产信创+英伟达’双芯架构” (two-chip design, home-made Xinchuang and Nvidia) 340; Linyi's city government, at a press conference on Dec 31, 2025, said its computing marketplace is adapted to “NVIDIA、华为昇腾等多种类型算力设备” (Nvidia, Huawei Ascend and other kinds of computing equipment) 341, and Jiangsu's data bureau, in a report of June 2026 with three partners, that the province's computing dispatch platform is adapted to Cambricon's and Huawei's chips and to “英伟达类型 A100、H800、V100 GPU” (Nvidia's A100, H800 and V100 GPUs) 342; and a weather-forecasting centre for the Greater Bay Area, on the Shenzhen meteorological bureau's site, asked on Sept 9, 2025, in a call for comment on its needs, for one card whose “核心型号为NVIDIA H200” (core model is the NVIDIA H200) 343; these are plans, a listing, a project begun, platforms and a call for comment, not purchases, and none says how the chips would be obtained. So the conflict ran through the state itself: its security and planning side against the companies, with the commerce side nearer to the companies.

18Guidance and public warnings, not a published ban. No published Chinese order against buying Nvidia chips was found in the 82 policy pages read. Such an order could have been found: the 2023 review conclusion telling operators of critical information infrastructure to stop buying Micron's products is among those pages, “应停止采购美光公司产品” 246. And when the finance ministry issued a purchase bar by name, on Jun 22, 2026, “不得采购46家美国企业（不包括在华美资企业）生产的产品” (government buyers may not buy products made by 46 American firms, leaving out American-funded firms in China), its list gave only the 46 firms' names, Lockheed Martin's first, and named none of the chip makers searched for (Nvidia, Intel, AMD, Micron, Qualcomm, in English and in Chinese) 344345. Nor was a published rule on importing these chips found. China's lists of goods that need an import licence, an automatic import licence or a dual-use import licence, for 2024, 2025 and 2026, read whole by script, name no computer, computer part or chip (no customs number under headings 8471, 8473 or 8542, and none of five words for them), though the same search finds the computers and chips that the dual-use list controls for export 346347348; nor do the lists of banned imports in force 349; the sixth batch, of 2005, lists 17 kinds of asbestos and chemicals 350. The licence lists set their scope by how the goods are named and described, each in a note of its own; the import-licence list's reads “进口许可证管理的商品范围以商品名称描述为准，商品编号供通关申报参考” 346, and no description names them either (each list with its saved copy: tables/import_lists_read.tsv). Nor does any customs or tariff text that a second search of Nov 25, 2026 reached and read whole: the announcements of the customs administration and of the Customs Tariff Commission found for January 2024 to November 2026, with their annexes, set no licence, hold or chip-specific inspection on importing these goods, and none names Nvidia or a chip model (the texts read: tables/customs_and_tariff_texts_read.tsv; not a census of every numbered announcement). Apart from the inspection of used goods on import, which reaches computers and integrated circuits of any origin (the list of 2015 of used goods inspected lot by lot at the port has “8542.3100-8542.9000 集成电路及微电子组件” (integrated circuits and microelectronic assemblies) 351, and a Shenzhen customs office fined an importer in July 2026 for graphics-card modules found to be used, “属于旧机电未报检” (used electromechanical goods not declared for inspection) 352), the one published import measure found that could reach American chips is a duty that goes by origin, “对原产于美国的所有进口商品，在现行适用关税税率基础上加征34%关税” 353 (from Apr 10, 2025), and the China Semiconductor Industry Association, on Apr 11, 2025, read customs' rule on the origin of integrated circuits as the place where the wafer was made: “根据海关总署的相关规定，‘集成电路’原产地按照四位税则号改变原则认定，即流片地认定为原产地” 354; by that rule a chip that an American firm designs and a foundry outside the United States makes is not of American origin. That retaliatory duty, 125% by the time of CNN's report of Apr 25, 2025, had, by import agencies that spoke to CNN, been waived, without an announcement, on eight kinds of American integrated circuits, memory chips left out: “tariffs on eight kinds of integrated circuits, covering most semiconductors except for memory chips, had been waived to zero”, while the customs administration and its offices in Shenzhen and Zhongshan “said they were not aware of the exemptions” 355; Fibocom Wireless, a maker of wireless modules, says the like of its own buying in its Hong Kong listing prospectus of Oct 14, 2025, its American-origin baseband and radio-frequency chips “獲豁免中國關稅” (exempt from Chinese tariffs) from Apr 24, 2025 and its American memory chips taxed, and names no announcement for the exemption 356. The purchase rules in point 2 bind Party and government bodies at township level and above and two kinds of public institution tied to them, and the same sentence adds “其他单位可不在采购需求中提出此项要求” (other units may leave this requirement out) 19. No published national plan found sets a home-made share for computing centres: every such share found is a province's or a city's. The national computing-network opinions of December 2023 set none, though they set one for green power, “国家枢纽节点新建数据中心绿电占比超过80%” (over 80% green power in new data centres at the national hubs) 357, and nor do seven later central texts read, up to the plan for AI and energy of April 2026, which asks to speed the fit of “自主智算芯片” (independent intelligent-computing chips) to home-made frameworks 358. Two national texts among the 82 policy pages do hold a home-made percentage, of other kinds: the 20% price preference of point 2, and an advisory committee's roadmap of October 2015 for Made in China 2025, which expected home-made products in several fields to reach stated shares of the home market by 2025, among them high-performance computers and servers at “国内市场占有率预计超80%” (expected to exceed 80% of the home market) and mobile-terminal chips at 40% 359; at its launch the industry ministry's vice minister said “路线图不是指令企业怎么做,而是指导性的、参考性的” (the roadmap does not tell firms what to do; it is guidance and reference) 360. A draft national plan for AI data centres with “at least 80 per cent of the core technology, AI chips included” from local suppliers was reported by Bloomberg in June 2026, from unnamed people 361. Asked on Jun 18, 2026 about foreign reports of large investment in a computing network, the state planner's Li Chao gave no investment figure and no home-made share, and said “在具体建设过程中，市场力量将起决定性作用；我们将会同有关部门，加强统筹协调和政策引导” (in the actual building, market forces will play the decisive role; we will, with the departments concerned, strengthen overall coordination and policy guidance) 362. Bloomberg's report of September 2024 said it in terms, by people it did not name: “The policy has taken the form of guidance rather than an outright ban” 274. The earliest reports saved of the summer's guidance said the same in August 2025 (point 14). The Financial Times's report of Sept 17, 2025 does call it a ban, in its headline, “China bans tech companies from buying Nvidia's AI chips”, and in its text, “The ban goes beyond earlier guidance from regulators”; what it describes is an order told to companies, on the word of three unnamed people. It reports no published rule, and none was found 283. Reuters's report of Nov 5, 2025 (point 15) has the word in its headline too, “China bans foreign AI chips from state-funded data centres, sources say”; its text speaks of guidance and of a directive, on the word of two unnamed people, and says “The sources did not identify which Chinese regulatory bodies had issued the order”. It, too, reports no published rule 286. The published texts found stop short of an order: the commentaries and the industry body's call in point 4 warn and urge, and none forbids. The state-media warnings came mostly in one burst: of 75 state and Party media items on Nvidia chips found from July 2025 to November 2026, 16 of the 19 that only warn about Nvidia's chips are dated Jul 31 to Sept 24, 2025, the first three of the day of the summons, the cyberspace regulator's notice as CCTV's site and Xinhua carried it and a Global Times report in which an expert says that deploying the chips in computing centres risks moving algorithms abroad, the last a Science and Technology Daily report of a forum at which an academician urged government and enterprise users to speed up home-made substitution 128125129; the three found later are in Global Times in March and November 2026, two an industry analyst's words in news reports and one the paper's own unsigned commentary (point 4), and the items after September 2025 are what two rounds of searches found, a sample of what these outlets printed and not a full count (tables/state_media_items.tsv). Before the burst, a search back to January 2024 found one item that only warns, of March 2025 (point 4); of the 20 warnings found from January 2024 to November 2026, 16 fall in those eight weeks. In every answer found on Nvidia's chips or on American AI chips for China, Beijing's spokespeople confirmed nothing (30 exchanges from December 2024 to Sept 13, 2026: a search of Nov 25, 2026 read every regular press conference of the foreign ministry in those months, 411 by its count, and the commerce ministry's regular briefings it could reach; a second search that day added six exchanges at the commerce ministry's briefings, and a second reading of the foreign ministry's pages, by a written rule, added two; each of the 30 answers is held and its words checked; and those since, below): “我不了解你提到的情况” (I am not aware of what you mention), said the foreign ministry on Aug 13, 2025 363 and the commerce ministry on Jan 22, 2026, asked whether the government had told companies it would approve H200 purchases only in special cases 364, and on Mar 19 and Jul 16, 2026 365366367; “具体问题建议向中方主管部门了解” (for specifics, I suggest you ask the competent Chinese departments), said the foreign ministry on Sept 18, 2025 368, and in like words on Jan 29 and Nov 9, 2026 369370. It had answered so before, on Aug 22, 2025, to a question that cited reports that China had stopped Chinese companies using the H20, “此前有报道称中方阻止中国公司使用H20芯片”: “这个问题建议向中方主管部门了解” (for this question, I suggest you ask the competent Chinese departments), neither confirming nor denying them 371. Asked on Nov 18, 2026 about Nvidia's statement that it would no longer supply Hopper data-centre products, the H200 among them, to China, the foreign ministry said only “中方此前已经多次阐明在美国输华芯片问题上的原则立场” (China has already stated many times its principled position on US chip exports to China) 372; asked on Nov 23 about the President's saying he would sell China only older chips, and whether China was negotiating with Washington, it said “关于美国出口管制措施，中方此前已表明立场” (China has already stated its position on US export-control measures) 373. A Hong Kong paper's account of a Financial Times report, Aug 19, 2026, says purchases “需通過國家發改委審批” (must pass the state planner's approval) 374: an approval step is reported, though no rule for it was found. A 2020 antitrust decision even obliges Nvidia to go on supplying its GPU accelerators to China on fair, reasonable and non-discriminatory terms, “向中国市场继续供应英伟达GPU加速器”, unless the conditions have since been lifted 375; the regulator's list of conditional decisions, read on Nov 24, 2026, has no notice changing or lifting them, while it carries notices lifting the conditions of two other deals and changing the party bound by a third 376. One listed company, 智微智能 (stock code 001339), which buys AI servers made in part by foreign makers, told the Shenzhen exchange in September 2026 that it had compared them with three Chinese catalogues, of technologies banned or restricted from import, of goods under import licence, and of dual-use items and technologies under import and export licence: “经比对，智算服务器及配套设备不涉及目录项下禁止或限制进口的产品或技术” (on comparison, the servers and their equipment are not products or technologies banned or restricted from import under the catalogues); the reply names no maker of the servers it buys or of their chips (it names AMD only as the platform of terminal products it develops, and names chip firms it has invested in), and puts the risk to supply on other countries' export controls 377. Two more listed companies told the same exchange the like in 2026. 宏景科技 (Hongjing Technology, 301396), in its reply of March 2026, said of its buying of computing servers that there was “不存在违反中国现行法律法规中关于进口、运营算力服务器的禁止性规定的情形” (no breach of the prohibitive provisions of Chinese law in force on importing or operating computing servers) 378. 首都在线 (Capital Online, 300846), in September 2026, said that it usually buys its GPU servers through suppliers in China, which handle their purchase, import and delivery, and that buying the servers for its computing-centre project that way “不违反中国现行法律法规的禁止性规定” (breaches no prohibitive provision of China's laws and regulations in force) 379. So Luckey's “China will not give them an import permit” 1 says more than the record shows: no permit rule for these chips was found in the import lists read (above). The law does allow a restriction outside the lists: the commerce ministry, with the State Council's approval and on the grounds the law lists, may “临时决定限制或者禁止前款规定目录以外的特定货物、技术的进口或者出口” (decide for a time to restrict or ban the import or export of particular goods and technologies outside the catalogues) 380, in the words of the law as amended in 2022; the law as revised on Dec 27, 2025, in force from Mar 1, 2026, keeps the power, “临时决定禁止或者限制前款规定目录以外的特定货物、技术的进出口” (decide for a time to ban or restrict the import and export of particular goods and technologies outside the catalogues), and among its grounds are national security and “为建立或者加快建立国内特定产业的” (to build, or speed the building of, a particular domestic industry) 381; no such decision naming these chips was found. Reuters's words are that Beijing “withholds approval for large-scale imports” 296. The 82 policy pages read are those of the project's note of policies checked, which has 103 entries on 83 saved pages; one of them, a Reuters report on MarketScreener (row 59), is a refusal page, and the report is read in a radio station's copy of the same article. What was found, then, is guidance, as the press reports it, and public warnings, not a published ban.

19Some purchases were approved, and some chips came in. Reuters, Jan 28, 2026: ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent “have been approved to purchase more than 400,000 H200 chips in total”, by four people it did not name 301. The same report says Beijing was “only granting approvals with conditions” and, by the same people, that these “were still being decided upon”; a set ratio of home-made chips for each purchase it gives only as “One proposal that Chinese authorities have discussed in the past” 301. The conditions are reported two ways. Lianhe Zaobao, the same day, citing an earlier Bloomberg report: Chinese regulators “要求上述三家科技企业需采购一定比例的国产晶片” (required the three companies to buy a share of home-made chips) as one condition 382. Bloomberg's report of Jan 23, by people it did not name, is of a step before purchase: regulators “granted in-principle approval for Alibaba, Tencent Holdings Ltd. and ByteDance Ltd. to move to the next stage of preparations for purchases” 383; its own Japanese excerpt says the authorities will urge companies to buy home-made chips, and sets no amount: “当局は承認の条件として、一定量の国産半導体チップ購入を企業に促す方針だが、具体的な数量は設定されていない” (as a condition of the approval, the authorities intend to urge companies to buy a certain amount of home-made chips, but no amount is set) 384. Three more things from those months bear on how far the approvals reached. Bloomberg had reported on Jan 8, 2026, from “people familiar with the situation”, that officials were preparing to let companies buy the chip “for select commercial use”, while “the H200 chip will be barred from the military, sensitive government agencies, critical infrastructure and state-owned enterprises due to security concerns” 385: a line drawn round state and security users. Nvidia's chief executive said the day after Reuters's report that no orders had followed: “我认为中国政府仍在决策中，我们尚未收到采购订单” (I think the Chinese government is still deciding; we have not yet received purchase orders), as Voice of America's Chinese service renders him 299. And when a senior executive vice president of Tencent, Dowson Tong, was asked on May 28, 2026 whether Beijing had approved Tencent's H200 purchases, he answered, in a Hong Kong news service's reported speech, “對於集團採購不同的芯片，中央政府是表示支持的” (as for the group buying different chips, the central government has said it supports this) 386; a Hong Kong paper's account of the same answer adds that he could not answer on particular chip models, “但就未可答复具体的芯片型号” 387. The Information reported on Jul 8, 2026, as the newsletter Trivium China relays it, that China planned to let its largest AI firms buy a limited number of Nvidia's H200 chips, “fewer than 200,000”, “less than half what the companies requested early this year”; in the newsletter's words, “The chips are for training only – with Chinese processors used for inference.”, and limited to processing “public data, not sensitive data such as Chinese customer information” 388. Reuters, Mar 17, 2026, by sources it did not name: “Nvidia has won Beijing's approval to sell its second-most powerful artificial intelligence chips to China” 389. Nvidia's report for the quarter to Jul 26, 2026 says the licensed sales “were restricted by the PRC government”, that it took a $0.4 billion charge “as the demand for H200 products diminished”, and then: “we have made a fraction of the allowed shipments under the USG's H200 licensing program” 224. The Financial Times, through Reuters, Aug 18, 2026: “Small batches of Nvidia's H200 chips, one of the company's most powerful AI chips, have been allowed to enter mainland China”; ByteDance and Tencent “have each received about 10,000 H200 processors in recent weeks”, while “Beijing wants them to keep the hardware outside mainland China”, and regulators told companies they could ship the chips to Hong Kong and use them there 303. The paper's own text, as Yahoo Finance carries it, says most of the chips, and gives the reason by one of its people: “Beijing wants them to keep most of these chips outside the mainland to support domestic chipmakers, one of the people said.” 390 Tencent's chief strategy officer, Mar 18, 2026, spoke of buying “higher-end imported GPUs which are now becoming available again” alongside more Chinese-designed ones 391; a Chinese record of the call's questions and answers has the same answer under his name, “采购重新恢复供应的高端进口GPU” (buying high-end imported GPUs whose supply has resumed) 392. Nvidia sold $2.4 billion of licensed Hopper products to China in the quarter ended Oct 25, 2026 (section 4) and said on its call: “we are no longer shipping licensed Hopper Data Center products to China” 304. It gave no reason; Reuters wrote that its then interim chief executive “did not explain why Nvidia had stopped shipping the processors or whether the halt would be permanent” 305. She told CNBC the same day that the licensed H200s “went to China within this last quarter” 320. Two saved news pages carry a statement of Nvidia's in their reports of the released licence table. The Standard of Hong Kong, in a page stamped Nov 20, 2026, 04:57 Hong Kong time, has Nvidia say “in a statement” that the approvals were “subject to strict conditions and limited volumes” and that “despite receiving some licenses, we have since stopped shipping H200s to China” 393. The Daily Caller, in a page stamped Nov 20, 2026, 20:29 UTC, about a day later, quotes an Nvidia spokesperson at more length (the page says the spokesperson spoke to the Daily Caller News Foundation): “The review and licensing process spanned more than half a year, with approvals subject to strict conditions and limited volumes.” and “Despite receiving some licenses, we have already stopped shipping H200s to China.” 394 Neither page says on which day the statement was made, and it gives no reason for the stop either. None of these says how many of those chips crossed into the mainland. Other Nvidia chips were sold, or were reported to have arrived, too. Nvidia's annual report, of the H20 licences of August 2025: “We generated approximately $60 million in H20 revenue under those licenses.” 243 iSoftStone's own record of its results meeting of Jun 25, 2026 says its Shanghai node built a new H20 inference cluster, “上海节点新建 H20 推理集群” 395; National Business Daily reported on Oct 13, 2026 that a person close to the company confirmed 8,000 Nvidia cards of the 6000D model had arrived 396; the company's own WeChat post in which the news first appeared was deleted, “推文内容已被软通动力删除” (the post has been deleted by iSoftStone), and, called by Cailian Press's reporter as an investor, “公司人士未正面回应” (the company's people did not answer directly) 397; a staff member told Yicai's reporter the same day that he was “对此事不太清楚” (not very clear about the matter) 398. Two universities posted notices to buy that card in July 2026, each holding suppliers to the government procurement law's conditions: Beihang, asking for quotes for four, “品牌 NVIDIA 规格型号 RTX PRO 6000D” 399, and Wuhan University, by competitive bidding, for the “国行合规RTX PRO 6000D型号” (the compliant China-market model) 400; a notice to buy is neither a purchase made nor a delivery. The older H20 was awarded in September 2025 and asked for by name in 2026: the University of the Chinese Academy of Sciences' award, published on its own purchasing site on Sept 28, 2025 and signed by the purchasing centre for central state bodies on Sept 26, lists six Dell servers, each with “8块GPU/FPGA/加速卡-NVIDIA HGX H20 8-GPU SXM 96GB 500W GPUs Assembly” 401, beside four servers each with “8*Ascend 910B4” 401; an award is a contract decision, not a delivery. Shanghai Jiao Tong University's procurement intention, signed May 9, 2026, as a tender site carries it, plans for July 2026 to rent “6~8 台英伟达 H20 八卡 GPU 服务器” 402 (six to eight eight-card Nvidia H20 servers); and Wenzhou University's procurement intention, signed Oct 27, 2026, plans “一台基于NVIDIA H20 GPU的高性能计算服务器” (one high-performance server based on the NVIDIA H20 GPU) for 1.04 million yuan, to be bought in December 2026 403; an intention is not yet a tender. Result notices of universities name other Nvidia models: Northwestern Polytechnical University's school of cyber security awarded on Jan 28, 2026 the rental of servers listed with “NVIDIA HGX H200 8-GPU SXM 141GB” modules, among others 404, and its school of management bought two cards, “NVIDIA H200 141G PCIe显卡2片”, for 874,700 yuan on Oct 21, 2026 405, both read in Internet Archive copies, as the schools' own pages no longer open; in November 2026 Fudan University bought four cards listed as “英伟达RTX Pro6000计算显卡” (Nvidia RTX Pro6000 compute cards) 406, and Southeast University two GPU cards whose model column reads H100, with the supplier's own name in the brand column 407. Each is a contract decision for a few cards or one server's rental, not a delivery. On the RTX PRO 5500, the card of the orders in point 7, The Information, through Reuters, Sept 27, 2026, by two people: the Chinese government “has signaled it could allow” some companies to buy the card; the industry ministry “recently asked companies including ByteDance and Alibaba to report their plans to purchase Nvidia's RTX PRO 5500 chips” and “told some Chinese companies the government intends to approve the purchases”; Reuters “could not immediately verify the report” 330. That is an intention reported, not an approval given. The Next Web's account of the same report has the ministry asking “how many of the chips they plan to buy, and what for” 408. Asked about the report, an Nvidia spokesperson said: “US firms continue to be restricted by a combination of outdated US export controls, which cover gaming products released nearly a half a decade ago, and China's own limits on US imports” 330. A paper of the Institute for Progress in Washington, dated Oct 19, 2026, which argues for limiting China's access to American AI chips, says of Chinese firms' orders for the card: “Chinese regulators are reportedly reviewing whether this demand is necessary” 409; it gives the Financial Times as its source for the orders, in the sentence before, and names none for this. That Financial Times report, of Oct 13, 2026, “China faces uphill battle to wean tech firms off Nvidia chips” 410, is held only in a Chinese translation by Taiwan's Economic Daily News; there one unnamed senior executive of a large Chinese technology company says the regulators are asking firms whether their demand for the card is really necessary, and expects the answer yes: “監管機構正與科技企業溝通，了解他們對輝達RTX Pro 5500的需求是否確有必要。坦白說，答案會是『有必要』。” (the regulators are talking with the technology companies to learn whether their demand for Nvidia's RTX Pro 5500 is really necessary; frankly, the answer will be that it is) 411. Reuters, Nov 23, 2026, by two people: some Chinese customers “have begun canceling orders” over concerns that the card “may not be permitted into China”, and “Reuters could not determine the scale of the cancellations or whether the US or China would restrict shipments”; by two people too, large purchases by internet companies such as ByteDance and Alibaba “could require Chinese government approval” 412. That report names no buyer, and none of the three listed buyers of point 7 had filed a delivery or a cancellation by Nov 25, 2026 (their notices on the disclosure site cninfo.com.cn, Oct 1 to Nov 25). By these reports and records, taken together, some purchases were approved and some chips came in.

20The companies had reasons of their own, and American curbs and conditions pushed too. Their own chips and costs first. Alibaba says its “chip design subsidiary T-Head has brought proprietary GPUs into scaled production” 413, and its chief executive said on the results call of Aug 20, 2026, in Chinese on the company's own record, that their wider use “避免了外购高端的商业化 GPU 的高额溢价对毛利的侵蚀” (has kept the high premium on bought high-end commercial GPUs from eating into gross profit) 414; he judged in November 2026, “结合当前形势” (in light of the present situation), that from 2027 on “公司绝大部分新增AI芯片供给将以自研平头哥国产芯片为主” (the great majority of the company's added AI chip supply will be mainly the home-made T-Head chips it develops itself), with other home-made chips bought for part of its inference 415; a transcript of the call on a stock-data site (where these two answers stand under the questioners' names, with no speaker label of their own) has that judgement after the words “在2027以及2028年，在2027之后” (in 2027 and 2028, after 2027), and, in the answer to a question on the new restrictions of the American commerce bureau, that in future most models “都会完全依赖于国产，国产芯片去做整个的训练” (will rely entirely on home-made chips for all their training) 416. Baidu's chief executive said in April 2025, in Reuters' words, that it had switched on a cluster “comprising 30,000 of its self-developed, third generation P800 Kunlun chips” 417. The founder of 360, Jul 23, 2025: its recent purchases “都是华为的产品” (are all Huawei's), and for inference “国产芯片性价比更高” (home-made chips are better value) than the H20; he granted that they trail Nvidia's but said they must be used, “只有咬牙坚持用” (only by gritting one's teeth and sticking with them) 418. Zhipu, on the US Entity List since Jan 16, 2025 419, says that in coding work the per-token inference cost on home-made chips “has reached a level comparable in cost-performance to mainstream imported GPUs”, and gives its own reason: “Given the persistent global supply constraints on compute, this path ensures that the Company's cost curve remains within its own control” 420. Huawei's rotating chairman gave the seller's reading of its buyers, in Huawei's summary of his roundtable of Sept 17, 2026: “For any customers, their decision to deploy Huawei solutions is made based on considerations about geopolitics and multi-vendor strategies. All companies must look to the long term, and try to ensure their supply security.” 312 China Daily, counted among the state media of point 4, in a report of Aug 12, 2025, in the weeks of those warnings, quoted an engineer at an internet company it did not name, and printed the words again on Aug 15: “Cybersecurity is of crucial importance to us. We cannot tolerate any potential risks. Even if H20 chips are available now, we are not that eager to buy.” 421422 Reuters, Sept 16, 2025, by two people it did not name: the RTX PRO 6000D (Reuters writes RTX6000D) met “only lukewarm demand”, being “seen as expensive for what it does”, in a report that also says “Beijing has become increasingly keen for Chinese firms to switch to domestic chips even if Nvidia's products are preferred by many companies” 282; the Financial Times, the next day, by three people it did not name: “Several companies had indicated they would order tens of thousands of the RTX Pro 6000D” 283. American curbs pushed too. Reuters reported in November 2023 that Baidu had ordered Huawei chips that August, ahead of new US curbs, “to prepare for a future where they may no longer be able to purchase from Nvidia” 423. Tencent's president, Martin Lau, said the same month of the new curbs: “And we will also try to look for domestic source for these training chips.” 424 Early in 2024, before the guidance The Information reported in May (point 13), the cut-down H20 met reluctance too: Reuters wrote on Feb 1, 2024, in its own words, that Huawei's 910B “has become more popular amid concern that buyers could be faced with further restricted access to Nvidia's products resulting from U.S. sanctions” 425, and, by the Wall Street Journal's report as Asia Financial relayed it in January 2024, “China’s Tencent and Alibaba told Nvidia that they will purchase only a few AI chips from the US chipmaker”, the two preferring home-made chips such as Huawei's 426. In November 2026 Tencent's president said the new US curbs on computing abroad left everyone in China with less advanced compute (point 9), and that they would hit training while, for inference, the “domestic supply chain should be able to cover it” 183; Kingsoft Cloud, on its call of Nov 24, 2026, in the interpreter's English as a stock-data site transcribes it, put itself outside their direct reach: “we currently do not operate computing resources overseas, and so we are not directly affected by the new rules” 427. Alibaba, in the results announcement dated Nov 19, 2026 that it filed in Hong Kong, wrote of the Commerce Department's interim rule of Nov 16, 2026 on remote access to advanced semiconductors, in the English version: “The Rule, as written, will impact our company's ability to access compute from certain providers outside China.” 428; the Chinese version of the same announcement has may where the English has will: “該規則（按現有條文）可能會影響本公司從某些中國以外提供商獲取計算資源的能力” 429 (the rule, as now written, may affect the company's ability to obtain computing resources from certain providers outside China). The US-listed Chinese companies whose annual reports speak of chip supply put the risk on export controls (point 11), and the three listed buyers of cards named PRO 5500 name overseas technology controls, and no Chinese approval, among the risks to delivery (point 7). A Chinese chip maker's prospectus says “地缘政治倒逼相关国内客户使用国产 GPU 产品” (geopolitics forces these domestic customers to use home-made GPUs), right after a list of American export rules 430. Hanna Dohmen, a research analyst at Georgetown University's CSET, in her written statement for a US-China commission hearing of Feb 6, 2025: “export controls are creating increased demand for indigenous Chinese equipment, fabrication capacity, and AI chips” 181. The commission's own report of November 2026, of the state: “U.S. and allied controls did not drive China's indigenization efforts, but they have added urgency in recent years.” 147 Nvidia, in every filing since November 2025: “we are already effectively foreclosed from the China market by U.S. export controls” 4. The H200 also met obstacles on the American side. The Commerce Department's rule of Jan 15, 2026 sets the conditions under which a licence application moves from a presumption of denial to case-by-case review; among them, the applicant must certify that “the item undergoes independent, third-party testing in the United States” and that “the aggregate shipments of the product to China and Macau will be no more than 50% of the total product shipped to customers for end use in the United States of that product” 186. Nvidia: chips shipped under the licences “are subject to a 25% tariff upon importation into the United States”, and “We have been unable to pass along any of the tariff to our customers” 224. Its spokesperson in February 2026, of the US licence conditions: “the conditions need to be commercially practical, else the market will continue to move to foreign alternatives” 431. Reuters, relaying the Financial Times, Feb 3, 2026: Chinese customers were “not placing H200 chip orders with Nvidia until it becomes clear whether they will be able to secure the licences” 432. And Washington returned without action the applications for 55% of the chips its summary of May 11, 2026 records, among them every application whose ultimate location is Malaysia, Indonesia or Thailand, the operator or consignee of each named for ByteDance, BytePlus, TikTok or Alibaba (section 4). Asked on Nov 20, 2026 whether he would let “these chips go to China”, the President said: “No, we want only older chips and we don't mind if none go, but only older.” (a commercial transcript) 433

21What Beijing meant by it is not settled. No Chinese document found states a motive for the unpublished guidance to companies, or ties an act against Nvidia to the trade talks. Beijing's own texts give purposes for its public acts: the cyberspace regulator summoned Nvidia “为维护中国用户网络安全、数据安全” (to safeguard Chinese users' cyber and data security) 222; an unsigned article on the work of the market regulator's Party group says the regulator's cases against Nvidia, Qualcomm and DuPont China “有力维护国家利益” (forcefully safeguarded national interests) (point 10) 225; and the regulator's annual report for 2025 lists the further investigation among cases that served “维护公平竞争” (to protect fair competition) 232. Several American bodies read parts of Beijing's conduct as leverage in the trade talks or as retaliation: the US-China commission in November 2025, which reads the finding against Nvidia as leverage; the White House on Nov 1, 2025, which speaks of retaliation against American chip makers and other major American companies and names none of them; and the Trade Representative's report of March 2026, which calls China's antimonopoly investigations retaliation (point 10) 227228230. Among China's commitments in the Busan deal the White House listed: “End Chinese retaliation against U.S. semiconductor manufacturers and other major U.S. companies.” 228 The Congressional Research Service, September 2025, read the pressure as a push for access to more advanced chips: China “may use its purported security concerns about the H20 to press for technology disclosures and access to more advanced chips, such as Nvidia's B30 chip”, and the market regulator's finding of that month was “another effort to pressure Nvidia to provide its advanced chips to China” 434. The Financial Times reported a request of another kind on Aug 10, 2025, by several people familiar with the matter, as a newsletter excerpts the report: “Chinese officials have told experts in Washington that Beijing wants the Trump administration to relax export restrictions on high-bandwidth memory (HBM) chips”, which, by people familiar with the matter, “seriously constrain the ability of Chinese companies, including Huawei, to develop their own AI chips” 435; and the Wall Street Journal, as Mint carries it, read Beijing the same way in its own words, on Aug 23, 2025: officials in Beijing were “reluctant to have Trump’s approval of the H20 treated as a U.S. concession to Chinese wishes”, and “They say that if the U.S. really wants to show goodwill, it will give Chinese users more access to cutting-edge American technology” 279; and on Nov 4, 2025, after the Busan meeting: “For Xi, the summit ended without achieving a key near-term objective: a concession on the U.S. chip ban.” 436 Senator Tim Kaine said on the Senate floor on Dec 18, 2025 that the administration had first claimed it would permit only “inferior chips, such as the Nvidia H20, not the H200”, “but China declined. They waited, believing that President Trump would fold, and that is what happened.” 437 He gave no source, and did not say whether by China he meant the government or the companies. The Trade Representative's words point the other way, in three interviews with Bloomberg, two on Bloomberg Television and one on a Bloomberg podcast (captions, not checked by ear). Of Washington's H200 decision: “That was not a negotiated outcome.” (Dec 19, 2025, 5:35) 438 Whether Chinese companies may buy is “a sovereign decision for China”, and “They're very committed to domestic production.” (May 15, 2026, 5:59 to 6:27) 8 Asked whether Xi raised chips on the President's trip: “But the Chinese side did not raise this issue with us.” (Nov 20, 2026, 0:28 to 0:34) 261 The Treasury Secretary gave a third reading on Aug 13, 2025. The premise was the interviewer's: asked about a report that Beijing was sending firms letters telling them to “shrug off those H20”, he did not confirm the letters and said “it it also tells me that they are worried about the Nvidia chips becoming the standard in China” (his words are from a caption track, not checked by ear) 439. The American side had put chips on the table itself: asked why the April block on the H20 was lifted, the Treasury Secretary said “You might say that that was a negotiating chip that we used in Geneva and in London” (a clip of his Bloomberg Surveillance interview that Bloomberg's Balance of Power played on Jul 15, 2025; captions, 8:55) 440; the same day the Commerce Secretary told Reuters, by CNN's report, that the resumed sales were part of the deal on rare earths: “We put that in the trade deal with the magnets” 441, though on Jun 11, 2025 the Treasury Secretary had told a Senate subcommittee “there is no quid pro quo in terms of chips for rare earths” 442; and the President wrote on Dec 8, 2025 that he had told Xi the H200 would be allowed: “President Xi responded positively!” 443 That is the President's account, not Xi's words. Whether the Nvidia case has ended is not known (point 10). As to the dates: the cyberspace regulator summoned Nvidia on Jul 31, 2025, two days after the Stockholm talks of Jul 28 and 29 222444; the antitrust finding came on Sept 15, the second day of the Madrid talks 223445. By my count over the figure's own list (tables/talks_and_acts.tsv), 7 of the 13 restrictive acts and words fall within 7 days of a meeting in person, where about a quarter of all days do; the easing acts and words do not cluster (2 of 9, one of the two being the scholar's interview of point 17). But restrictions also followed deals: the order on state-funded data centres was reported on Nov 5, 2025, six days after the Busan meeting 286446, and the planner's guidance on home-made chips and the AI-chip evaluation list came a week and more after the Beijing summit of May 14, 2026 12422447. Some dates are those of press reports, the acts come in bursts, and a count of dates cannot show a motive. None of the 40 saved official pages on the talks (the two sides' readouts, joint statements and fact sheets, and six notices of a round to come) names Nvidia, the H20 or the H200, though the same search finds export controls in some; nor do the President's own posts on four calls with Xi and on two rounds. One Chinese official text sets the chip beside the talks: asked on Jul 18, 2025 about American officials' remark that approving H20 sales was part of the trade negotiations, the commerce ministry's spokesperson first described what followed the London talks and then said “美方近日又主动表示将批准对华销售英伟达H20芯片” (the US side has recently also said, of its own accord, that it will approve sales of Nvidia's H20 chips to China); the answer neither confirms nor denies that the chip was part of the talks 448. Earlier, on Jun 2, 2025, the ministry's spokesperson had named American chip measures among those that, it said, broke the consensus of the Geneva talks, “包括发布AI芯片出口管制指南、停止对华芯片设计软件（EDA）销售” (among them issuing guidance on export controls for AI chips and stopping sales of chip-design software (EDA) to China), “严重破坏日内瓦经贸会谈既有共识” (seriously undermining the consensus already reached at the Geneva talks) 449: it counts America's chip measures, not China's own acts, against the talks' consensus. Under either reading the direction stands: what is open is what Beijing wanted from its acts, not which way they pulled the companies.

6. What can be said, and how

  • Flat, on documents and filings. China's declared policy is to end dependence on foreign core technology and to raise the share of home-made chips, leaving room, in the leader's own words, for imported technology that is secure and controllable (points 1 to 3). Its regulators opened a case against Nvidia and summoned it (point 10). Nvidia told securities regulators in November 2025 that the Chinese government “restricted customers from purchasing, importing, or using our data center products” (the annual report says “discouraged” 243), and, two sentences later, that “we are already effectively foreclosed from the China market by U.S. export controls” 4. Alibaba told them the cyberspace regulator asked companies to halt purchases of certain chips (point 11). A Shanghai government body says internet companies' clusters in the city run mainly on Nvidia (point 5). By May 11, 2026 Washington had approved 879,868 of Nvidia's and AMD's chips, none yet shipped, and returned without action the applications for 1,888,950 (section 4). Nvidia's revenue from customers based in China fell by about two thirds during 2025 and was, in the quarter ended Oct 25, 2026, the highest of the nine quarters reported on that basis; that is not a count of chips entering China (section 4).
  • In public, as warnings and urging, not as orders. The security ministry's warning, the state-media items that only warn (16 of the 19 found are dated Jul 31 to Sept 24, 2025; the items after September 2025 are what two rounds of searches found, a sample and not a full count), the state broadcaster's account saying buyers could choose not to buy the H20, and an industry body's call for caution (points 4 and 18). Nothing read ties the state-media texts to the guidance the press reports, and a Party paper also printed a scholar's advice to stock imported chips (point 17).
  • As reported, from unnamed people. Who gave the instructions to stop buying, and when; the warning about traitors; the customs checks; the 600,000 chips Reuters reported in Hong Kong; every number in the first table of section 4 except the United States government's count and Nvidia's own. Some of these rest on one outlet and some on more, each with its own sources: the warning about traitors on one (the Wall Street Journal), the port checks of October 2025 on one (the Financial Times), the rule for state-funded data centres on one (Reuters), the split inside Beijing on one (the Financial Times) and the 600,000 on one (Reuters); the pressure against the H20 in August 2025 on five, though they differ on whether firms were told to stop; the instruction on the RTX PRO 6000D on two (the Financial Times and Bloomberg); the H200 held by customs in January 2026 on two (Reuters and the South China Morning Post); and the go-ahead of late January 2026 on three, which Nvidia's chief executive disputed the next day.
  • As others read it. That Beijing's acts were leverage in trade talks, or a sovereign choice with no bargain in them (point 21).
  • Not to be said. That China banned Nvidia chips: no published ban was found, and some chips came in. That an import permit was refused: no permit rule for these chips was found in China's published import lists of 2024 to 2026 (point 18). That Beijing spoke with one voice: its commerce side and its security and planning side pulled apart (point 17). That the companies wanted only Nvidia: they also build and buy home-made chips. That the antitrust case shows Beijing wanted no Nvidia sales: by Nvidia's account the finding is that it offered Chinese customers degraded products. That the case was a bargaining move, or that it was not: both are readings (point 21). That only Beijing stood in the H200's way: Washington's conditions and a 25% tariff did too, and Washington returned without action the applications for 55% of the chips its summary of May 11, 2026 records. That Washington refused the applications it returned: the table gives no reason.

One sentence that survives all five limits. From 2024 the planning side of the Chinese state and the industry ministry, and from 2025 its security side, pressed China's AI companies, through unpublished guidance that the press reported, to buy home-made chips rather than Nvidia's, and from the summer of 2025 to stop ordering them, while the security ministry warned in public of backdoors in chips made abroad and state media warned about Nvidia's chips, most of all in August and September 2025; other parts of the state welcomed Nvidia and, by the reports, approved some purchases; the companies went on ordering Nvidia chips, in the millions by the press reports, and by those reports had received a small part of them by late 2026; they were also turning to home-made chips, for reasons of their own and under American curbs; and whether Beijing meant its acts as lasting policy or as leverage in trade talks is read both ways.

What this note leaves open

  • Palmer Luckey's clip has not been checked by ear, nor have the Trade Representative's words of Nov 20, 2026 (1:06 to 1:35), where the two caption tracks differ on one word and on where a sentence breaks.
  • Five South China Morning Post articles are held only in their first paragraphs; none is cited in this note. Articles of The Information and Bloomberg are held through outlets that carry or relay them; Bloomberg's report of Jan 23, 2026 only in its English opening and its own Japanese excerpt, and its report on the RTX PRO 5500 (point 7) only in its own Japanese excerpt of Nov 4, 2026; other outlets' accounts of that report are saved and are not cited. Five Financial Times articles of 2025 cited here were saved by hand from the paper's own site on Nov 23, 2026 and read whole. The paper's report of Aug 10, 2025 on high-bandwidth memory is held only as a newsletter excerpts it 435, its report of Aug 12, 2025 only as a mirror of a syndicated copy, its report of November 2025 as The Irish Times carried it, its report of Feb 3, 2026 only as Reuters relayed it 432, and its report of August 2026 on deliveries as Yahoo Finance Canada carries the paper's text 390 and as Reuters and a Hong Kong paper relayed it. Its report of Oct 13, 2026 on the RTX PRO 5500 (point 19) is held only in a Taiwanese paper's Chinese translation; its own page, as the Internet Archive holds it, is a registration page.
  • The Commerce Secretary's words are from a commercial transcript; a search found the same sentence in the committee video's own captions (1:17:28 to 1:17:47; a reading, with no saved copy). The committee's page for the hearing offers the video and his prepared remarks, and no transcript 258, as again on Nov 24, 2026 450; govinfo's list of the 409 Senate hearing prints of the 119th Congress issued or changed since Apr 22, 2026 holds none of this hearing 451.
  • Nvidia's report for the quarter ended Oct 25, 2026 was not yet filed when looked for on Nov 23, 2026: the commission's record of the company's filings, fetched that day, lists none for that quarter 452, nor did it when fetched again on Nov 24, 2026, with filings up to Nov 23 453, or on Nov 25, 2026 at 22:16 UTC 454. Its commentary on that quarter says: “We plan to file our 10-Q in the coming weeks.” 455
  • What Beijing meant (point 21): no Chinese official text found states a motive for the unpublished guidance or ties an act against Nvidia to the trade talks (the purposes stated for its public acts are security, national interests and fair competition). A Chinese news site's commentator did tie one to them: Guancha, on Aug 7, 2025, quoted its technology commentator, whom it does not name, as saying of the summons that “这一约谈时机还增添了中国在中美贸易谈判中的筹码” (its timing also added to China's bargaining chips in the China-US trade talks) 456, a commentator's reading, not the state's. No American written readout of the rounds in London, Madrid, Kuala Lumpur, Paris and Korea, of the round of September 2026, of the two leaders' own meeting in Shenzhen (the White House posted only the three leaders' declaration and a trip summary) or of the video calls of Dec 5, 2025 and Apr 30, 2026 was found in the Treasury's indexes of readouts and press releases, the Trade Representative's monthly lists of press releases or the White House's lists of its posts, as read on Nov 24, 2026; the Treasury's notices before four rounds and the President's posts after two name no chips. The foreign ministry's list of news on the United States names five calls between Xi and the President from January 2025 to November 2026; both sides' accounts of all five are saved, and none names chips. Why Washington returned applications without action: its table gives no reason.
  • The window-guidance paper said to circulate in October 2025 (point 15): no text, issuer, number or date was found. The reported draft plan with 80% home-made technology (point 18) has not been published, and the planner, asked, did not confirm it.
  • Local rules: the official page of Binjiang's rule of 2026 did not open, though the district political consultative conference's site carries the same measures word for word 119; Sichuan's measures of 2026 were first held only as a news site carries their text; the provincial commission's own page is now held as the Internet Archive captured it 82. Tianjin's voucher rule is held as Sina carries it 457, and the National Data Administration's site carries the same text with its signing date, Apr 27, 2025 458; of five cities' voucher rules sought, Suzhou's and Wuhu's were reached, Nanjing's only through an application guide, Qingyang's only through press accounts, and Chengdu's at first only through press accounts and then in a consultancy's full copy of its revised rules, compared by eye with the bureau's signed file, a scan 72; a search of Nov 25, 2026 across the 31 province-level governments and 24 named cities and new areas added the rules listed in rows 55 to 102 of tables/local_vouchers_more.tsv, four searches of the same day that went city by city, each with a second pass, and readings of named and earlier rules added rows 103 to 216, a later reading of the same day added row 217 (Dalian's draft AI measures of April 2025, a draft, not counted), and none reached the text of Chongqing's measures of August 2025, held only as their issuing notice in an Internet Archive capture 459; the state planner's national voucher has no text of its own found; Zhejiang's development and reform commission, in its notice for 2026 payouts as a community account on Sohu reprints it, says “人工智能券（算力券）是国家使用超长期特别国债资金” (the AI voucher, or computing voucher, is a policy tool for which the state uses ultra-long special treasury bond money, as the sentence goes on to say), for card types including “所有自主智算算力及部分高性能非自主智算算力” (all independent intelligent computing and some high-performance intelligent computing that is not independent) 460, and Tianjin's rule names as its basis the national computing-network opinions of December 2023, which say “鼓励面向中小企业发放算力券、运力券” (encourage issuing vouchers for computing and for network capacity to small and medium-sized firms) 357.
  • Public trade figures do not show how many of these chips entered China: each customs line is a total for a kind of goods and names no maker (yearly sums for five places in tables/trade_annual_summary.tsv). No saved page says under which line an importer in China or Hong Kong declares the chips. Nvidia's own classification list, “provided for informational purposes only”, gives the subheadings it applies itself: 8471.80 for its HGX H200 and HGX H20 parts, 8473.30 for its H200 NVL and H20 NVL16 parts and 8471.50 for its DGX H200 systems 461323. Singapore's own tables, read through its statistics service and not saved among the sources, show re-exports to mainland China under one line for computer units of about S$3.0 billion in July to October 2026, against about S$18 million in the first half; they name no goods or maker.
  • The fuller record is in two files beside this one: OFFICIAL_RECORD.md (government documents and filings) and TIER2_REPORTING.md (reporting).
